# Code Block

Yep. Here are **5 LeetCode array problems** where the constraint is what makes the problem nasty. I’d do them in roughly this order.

### 1. LeetCode 525 — Contiguous Array

**Difficulty:** Medium
**Constraint:** Binary array, equal `0`s and `1`s.

> Find the longest contiguous subarray containing an equal number of `0` and `1`.

**Think about:** prefix sums + changing the meaning of `0`.

---

### 2. LeetCode 560 — Subarray Sum Equals K

**Difficulty:** Medium
**Constraint:** Elements can be negative, so normal sliding window doesn't work.

> Count the number of contiguous subarrays whose sum equals `k`.

Example:

```text
nums = [1, 2, 3], k = 3
answer = 2
```

**The trap:**
You might instinctively try two pointers. **Don't.** Negative numbers destroy the monotonicity you need.

**Think about:**
"If my prefix sum is `X` here, what prefix sum did I need to have earlier?"

---

### 3. LeetCode 152 — Maximum Product Subarray

**Difficulty:** Medium
**Constraint:** Negative numbers completely change the game.

> Find the contiguous subarray with the largest product.

Example:

```text
[2, 3, -2, 4] → 6
```

But:

```text
[-2, 3, -4] → 24
```

**The nasty part:**
A negative number can turn the **smallest product into the largest product**.

So ask:

> At every position, do I need to remember only the maximum product?

---

### 4. LeetCode 907 — Sum of Subarray Minimums

**Difficulty:** Medium
**Constraint:** Every possible subarray has a minimum, and there can be O(n²) of them.

> Find the sum of the minimum value of every contiguous subarray.

Example:

```text
[3,1,2,4]

Subarrays:
[3]       → 3
[3,1]     → 1
[3,1,2]   → 1
[3,1,2,4] → 1
[1]       → 1
[1,2]     → 1
[1,2,4]   → 1
[2]       → 2
[2,4]     → 2
[4]       → 4

answer = 17
```

**The key realization:**
Don't enumerate subarrays.

Instead ask:

> **For each element, how many subarrays have this element as their minimum?**

This is where **monotonic stacks** enter.

---

### 5. LeetCode 42 — Trapping Rain Water

**Difficulty:** Hard
**Constraint:** You need to determine how much water exists at every position without repeatedly scanning left/right.

Example:

```text
height = [0,1,0,2,1,0,1,3,2,1,2,1]

answer = 6
```

The obvious observation is:

```text
water at i = min(max_left, max_right) - height[i]
```

But calculating `max_left` and `max_right` naively can get expensive.

**Your challenge:** find an **O(n)** solution.

There are actually multiple good approaches here:

* prefix/suffix maximum
* two pointers
* monotonic stack

---

### My recommended attack order

```text
525 → 560 → 152 → 42 → 907
```

And I would **not look at solutions** for these yet.

For each one, first try to answer three things:

1. **What makes brute force too slow?**
2. **What information from the previous elements could I reuse?**
3. **What does the constraint prevent me from doing?**

If you want the most brutal one **right now**, take **#907**.
